# TO THE SHUT PALACE
# OF THE KING;

## BY AN ANONYMOUS PHILOSOPHER
## KNOWN AS THE LOVER OF TRUTH <note>original: "Philaletha." This is the pseudonym of Eirenaeus Philalethes, an influential and mysterious alchemist whose identity—likely George Starkey—remained a secret for centuries.</note>.

# DEDICATION.

<warning>A large damp stain is visible in the lower center of the page.</warning>

*...that it belongs to another, I willingly confess; but you will grant pardon, since in this regard I am primarily looking to my own humble means, being conscious that I could draw nothing worthy of your virtues and mental gifts from my own small shelves. Those whose household furnishings are meager have need of borrowed equipment. <note>A classical idiom suggesting that when one lacks personal wealth or innate genius, they must rely on the resources or wisdom of others.</note> Yet you will not despise it, since even the Gods are appeased by those whose offerings are obtained from elsewhere, out of the Sabaean harvest. <note>Sabaea (modern Yemen) was famous in antiquity for its incense and frankincense. The author argues that even a gift bought from others is acceptable to the divine.</note> Especially since this charming little book seems to have been born in the very bosom of favoring Nature, with Sincerity <note>original: *Candore*—this refers to purity of mind or scholarly integrity.</note> acting as midwife; and so, it cannot fail to be—indeed, it ought to be—most welcome to you, whom Nature long ago enrolled among her closest friends. Your home has hitherto stood open to the entire retinue of the Muses and the Graces <note>The Muses represented the arts and sciences, while the Graces represented charm and social beauty.</note>, like some Parnassus or Helicon <note>Mountains in Greece sacred to the Muses; the author is calling Vogt's home a sanctuary for scholars.</note>. Let it lie open, and let it stand open to this work of ours, which does not come forth lean or begging from the cave of Trophonius <note>The Cave of Trophonius was a Greek oracle; legend says those who entered emerged pale and unable to laugh. Langius means his book is not gloomy or obscure.</note>; but rather with a truly composed manner, an open brow, and a translucent toga, from the innermost*

# DEDICATION.

...from the inner sanctuaries <note>original: *adytis* — the most sacred, private chamber of a temple, used here to suggest the hidden secrets of alchemy.</note> of Chemistry <term>Chymia</term> <note>In the 17th century, "Chymia" represented the overlap of what we now call chemistry and the philosophical traditions of alchemy.</note> it comes to you, having been summoned. Therefore, kindly admit it; for if you receive it properly, I would not doubt that this grateful guest will render you honored with its gifts. I pray to **GOD** that for your sake, for your kin, for Nature, and for us, you may long flourish and endure for the public hope.

At Hamburg, on the Ides of December <note>December 13th.</note> in the year of the common era 1666 <note>original: *CIƆ IƆ C LXVI* — an older Roman numeral notation for 1000, 500, 100, 66.</note>.

<meta>This page transitions from the formal dedication signed by Johannes Langius to the Editor’s Preface. While the dedication was addressed to a specific patron, this preface addresses the general reader to explain why this specific alchemical treatise is being published.</meta>

<page-type>preface</page-type>
<header>THE EDITOR’S PREFACE</header>

### TO

# T H E  R E A D E R .

When not long ago I obtained this little treatise—which I here deliver to the public—from a certain most Excellent Man who is most learned in these matters, *Kind Reader*, it immediately pleased me so much that, as if in the arena and "on a standing foot" <note>original: *stante pede* — a Latin idiom meaning "immediately" or "without delay."</note>, I formed the plan to share it for your benefit. I was driven to this because the Author seemed to be almost the only genuine preparer and Possessor of the Physical Tincture <term>Tinctura Physica</term> <note>Also known as the Philosopher's Stone or the Elixir, this was the ultimate goal of alchemy, believed to transform base metals into gold and heal all diseases.</note> of this age in which we live. Furthermore, he composed his writing for the purpose that, once published, he might be recognized as an Adept <term>Adeptus</term> <note>In alchemical tradition, an "Adept" is a master who has successfully discovered the secret of the Great Work.</note>, and those wandering aimlessly in the Art might be brought back to the right path. 

I was also moved by the candor and clarity <note>original Greek: *σαφήνεια* (saphēneia) — meaning lucidity or transparency of expression.</note> of the writing itself, and by a certain continuous tone reminiscent of Sendivogius <note>Michael Sendivogius (1566–1636), a world-renowned Polish alchemist whose writings were considered essential for any serious student of the craft.</note>, whom the Author follows closely at every step—and not infrequently even excels. He certainly always explains the material excellently, even when not following a formal instructional method. I was confident, therefore, that by publishing this, both the Author’s most praiseworthy intention would be satisfied, and it would benefit as many as possible, who even today...

<meta>catchword: or (original Latin: *aut*)</meta>

<summary>The editor introduces a newly discovered alchemical treatise, praising its anonymous author as a true "Adept" whose clarity rivals or exceeds the famous Michael Sendivogius. The editor hopes the work will guide those struggling to understand the secrets of the "Physical Tincture."</summary>
<keywords>Editor's Preface, Alchemy, Michael Sendivogius, Physical Tincture, Adept, Chemical Art, Johannes Langius</keywords>

--- Page 13 ---
<meta>This page continues the Editor's Preface. The previous page ended mid-sentence, describing how the author explains the alchemical material excellently so that seekers might find their way.</meta>

<page-type>preface</page-type>
<header>The Editor's Preface to the Reader.</header>

...[so that] a light might be kindled in that moonless night of errors for those who lie in wait for the Great Philosopher’s Stone <term>Lapis Philosophorum</term>—whether through wretched labor, or presumptuous knowledge, or persistent sighs—even though, alas! their hopes are repeatedly cut short. 

For even though, for a few years now, a great many have laboriously sought the title of "True Philosophy" and have presumed to teach—or more truly, I should say, to drive insane—the world with their fantastical books under the mask of a forged name; and though they have even exposed the ignorance of several men (and those of no obscure rank) onto the stage, not without the laughter of the more learned; nevertheless, to more disciplined minds and to those who, as the saying goes, have conversed more familiarly with the "dead philosophers" <note>The "dead philosophers" refers to the study of classic, ancient texts rather than contemporary, potentially fraudulent ones.</note>, their frauds and hooked tricks were immediately apparent. 

This went so far that these "barley-fed" <term>hordeatici</term> <note>original: *hordeatici* — literally "barley-men." Historically referring to gladiators fed on cheap barley, the editor uses it here to describe low-quality, cheap, or fraudulent pretenders.</note> promisers, who spoke with such a boastful mouth, were a little later exposed before their admirers—like the donkey in the lion's skin—and being abandoned and left to their own devices, they have thus far paid the penalty of fools <term>blacennomion</term> <note>original: *blacennomion* — a rare term derived from Greek, referring to the "price of stupidity" or the behavior of a simpleton.</note> among the forgers. 

But this author, whom we now present for the common benefit, preserves the style and the thread of the noble philosophers everywhere, as if following a straight line from the start...

# The Editor's Preface

...and breathes a heartfelt Chemistry <term>Chymia</term> <note>original: *cordialemque Chymiam*. The editor suggests the work is not merely technical but comes from the heart or essence of the craft.</note> from every side, and reveals itself abundantly by its own signs; so I hope it will happen that, once this light has arisen, the patched-together falsehoods of others will finally be utterly dissipated like mist and smoke. Truly, who or of what kind the Author of such a youthful <note>original: *mustulentæ* — literally "smelling of new wine" or "must." It suggests the work is fresh, vigorous, and perhaps produced by a young mind.</note> little work might be, I know as little as the most ignorant; nor, since he wished his own name to remain hidden, would I think it right to inquire against his will. 

Yet it is clear enough that he is well-beloved by **G O D**, like a certain Daniel, and is a true "man of desires" <note>A reference to Daniel 10:11, implying a person of great spiritual longing to whom God reveals secrets.</note> (if indeed he still survives and breathes the vital air); he is one who has attained the Medical, Chemical, and Physical secrets of the Philosophers at an age when many others are scarcely allowed to know the mere surface of these things, and then only as if through a lattice window <note>original: *per transennam* — a metaphor for seeing something partially or indirectly.</note>. 

Certainly, one who is nearly his equal and who has unearthed this art from books at such a young age will be found very rarely. Thomas Norton, an Englishman <note>Thomas Norton (c. 1433–1513), a famous alchemist and author of *The Ordinal of Alchemy*.</note>, professes that he knew the White Tincture <term>Tinctura alba</term> <note>The alchemical substance capable of transmuting base metals into silver.</note> in the twenty-eighth year of his age; likewise, Helmont <note>Jan Baptist van Helmont (1580–1644), a pioneer of chemistry.</note> records how Theophrastus Paracelsus <note>The famous 16th-century physician and occultist.</note> received the Stone of Gold-making <term>Lapis chrysopæus</term> <note>The Philosopher's Stone in its aspect of creating gold.</note> around that same year; so too Dionysius Zacharias <note>A 16th-century French alchemist known for his autobiographical accounts of the work.</note>, and perhaps a few others, being young men...

<meta>catchword: titi</meta>

<summary>The editor praises the anonymous author's youthful brilliance in alchemy, comparing his early mastery of chemical secrets to famous figures like Thomas Norton and Paracelsus.</summary>
<keywords>alchemy, Philosopher's Stone, White Tincture, Thomas Norton, Paracelsus, Helmont, Dionysius Zacharias, chemical secrets</keywords>

...-tieth year <note>original: *tertii feruntur* — completing *tertio* or *tertii* from the previous page. The editor is comparing ages: Norton at twenty-eight, others at thirty, but this author at only twenty-three.</note>; but these were led by teachers and borrowed wisdom <note>original: *alienâ Minervâ* — literally "by another's Minerva," a Latin idiom for using someone else's intellect or instructions.</note>. This is not so much a cause for wonder, as one can still praise their capable minds for being receptive. 

But to gather roses from the most thorny thistles and brambles of writers <note>A common metaphor for navigating the confusing, intentionally obscured, and difficult literature of alchemy.</note> at the age of twenty-three—without a teacher and by one's own guidance—and to produce the true <term>Philosophers' Stone</term> <note>The legendary substance of the "Great Work," capable of transmuting base metals into gold and acting as a universal medicine.</note>, has perhaps been granted to no one, or as has been said, to only the rarest few until now. Who would deny that hidden knowledge has rained down from **GOD** into such an intellect in a singular way, through open conduits <note>original: *peroledis*, likely a corruption of *proluviis* (floods/overflows) or *canalibus* (channels), which fits the metaphor of divine knowledge "raining down."</note>? 

I believe thousands could be counted who have been occupied in this study and "race-course" <note>original: *stadioque* — referring to the long, competitive, and exhausting pursuit of alchemical truth.</note> until advanced old age, yet were unable to even cross the first thresholds of this Wisdom, even though many have toiled for fifty or sixty years, more or less, through every conceivable hardship. 

All the more reason for us to revere the illustrious <note>original Greek: *ὁ πάνυ* (*ho pany*) — a title of high respect for an author.</note> Author; for though he was generously showered with such great and unusual gifts <note>original: *charismatis* — spiritual graces or divinely inspired talents.</note> from **GOD**, he did not wish to sing only to himself in private like the **Aspendian Cither-player** <note>A classical proverb referring to an ancient musician who allegedly played so quietly that only he could hear it; it describes someone who keeps their talents or knowledge to themselves.</note>. Instead, what he had learned without envy, he has kindly wished to share without envy. Which, among most others...

<summary>The editor marvels at the author's ability to master alchemy at the age of twenty-three without a teacher, a feat rarely achieved even by those who study for sixty years. # Editor's Preface

...until now. It is pointless to recount here how much envy has prevailed in handing down these secrets, or how much malice has hedged in their writings as if with a partition wall; any seeker of chemistry will be able to testify to this well enough. Indeed, <note>original Greek: οἱ τῶν Φιλοσόφων παῖδες — "the sons of the Philosophers," a traditional term for alchemists.</note> **the sons of the Philosophers** considered it a point of pride to mock posterity in this way, and they triumphed, as it were, behind a veil of envy and chimerical deceptions. Thus, as is found in Scaliger’s edition of the Greek *Chronicle of Eusebius*, Democritus of Abdera "wrote about gold, silver, stones, and purple <note>original Greek: λοξῶς (*loxos*) — meaning "slantingly," "ambiguously," or "indirectly," like the riddles of an oracle.</note> **obscurely**." Likewise, a certain "Maria, a wise Hebrew woman" <note>Maria the Jewess, a semi-legendary alchemist of late antiquity, often credited with inventing the *balneum Mariae* or "bain-marie" (water bath).</note> and others were praised by Ostanes because "they hid the art in many wise <note>original Greek: αἰνίγμασι (*ainigmasi*)</note> **riddles**." However, they condemned Pammenes for "writing <note>original Greek: ἀφθόνως (*aphthonos*) — literally "without envy," meaning he was too clear and generous with his secrets, which was considered a betrayal of the alchemical guild.</note> **lavishly**."

But our author, having been taught by a better spirit, has separated the chemical art from all common errors. Having struck down the sophistries and exploded the curious dreams of those who merely think themselves wise, he describes his findings so candidly, clearly, and transparently that you will find no one else who explains so many things more clearly—even if <note>original Greek: Ἀλήθεια (*Aletheia*) — the personification of Truth.</note> **Truth** herself were speaking. Indeed, even if one considers what Mary Rante, an Englishwoman, promised <note>original: *ἀποκαλυπτικῶς* (*apokalyptikos*) — "revelatorily" or "in the manner of a prophecy."</note> **apocalyptically** regarding the making of gold in the year sixteen hundred and sixty-

<meta>catchword: primo (first)</meta>

<summary>The editor discusses the traditional secrecy and "envy" of alchemists like Democritus and Maria the Jewess, contrasting their obscure riddles with the remarkable clarity and honesty of the current author.</summary>
<keywords>alchemy, secrecy, Democritus of Abdera, Maria the Jewess, Mary Rante, alchemical riddles, clarity, 17th-century chemistry</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the Editor's Preface. ->### To the Reader.<-

...at first that it would become common, or finally that events—even after a <term>lustrum</term><note>A period of five years.</note> has passed—should confirm it; I have no doubt that one could take an opportunity from this. He follows in the footsteps of **Sendivogius**, but with both a better order and far more accurate distinctions. Granting that he may have taught more obscurely than Sendivogius regarding the true <term>Subject of the Philosophers</term><note>The "Subject" refers to the *materia prima* or the base substance used to begin the alchemical transformation into the Philosopher's Stone.</note>; (I say "more obscurely" with respect to beginners, who might perhaps learn more easily from **Sendivogius**;) yet certainly, to those who look more carefully and without the bias of a preconceived opinion, a Sun clearer than that of Sendivogius himself cannot help but rise. In other matters, however, neither Sendivogius nor anyone else—whether from the **Ancients** or from subsequent centuries—would dare to claim superiority! Oh, how separate are the streams of <note>original: *Merrhæ & Siloam*</note> **Marah and Siloam**! <note>Marah (Exodus 15:23) had bitter, undrinkable waters, while Siloam (John 9:7) was a pool associated with healing and sight. The editor uses this biblical metaphor to contrast the "bitter," confusing writings of other alchemists with the "healing," clear instructions of this author.</note> What a great disparity there is between these and other writings, which until now have paved the way to precipices for a world groping in the dark! If only it had pleased the excellent Author to commit his most polished writings to the public press himself—for I hear he has also completed a **Dialogue on the liquor of Water-Fire or Alcahest** <note>The *Alcahest* was the hypothesized "universal solvent" of the Paracelsian alchemists, capable of reducing any body into its primary liquid form.</note>—I have no doubt we would possess everything in a more correct and absolute form; Ne-

<meta>catchword: ut</meta>

<summary>The editor compares the author’s work favorably against Michael Sendivogius, claiming that while it may seem obscure to novices, it offers far greater clarity and accuracy for the true seeker. He contrasts this work's "healing waters" with the confusing "bitter waters" of previous alchemical texts and mentions a lost or unreleased dialogue on the Alcahest.</summary>

--- Page 18 ---
<meta>This page continues the Editor's Preface. The editor is explaining the textual interventions he had to make due to the poor quality of the manuscript source, emphasizing his desire for accuracy while hoping the original author might eventually provide a perfected version.</meta>

-># Editor’s Preface<-

Although I have attended to this most scrupulously, yet, because it happened that I used a manuscript that was not entirely correct (as is commonly the case), I would not dare to assert that everything corresponds most rigidly to the Author's words. For quite often, because the sense was openly gaping <note>The editor means the meaning was incomplete or nonsensical due to errors in the handwritten source.</note>, it was necessary to provide help by changing one or two little letters. Nevertheless, we have studiously taken care that wherever some consistent meaning could be drawn out, we would not disturb anything rashly. 

We vow—and, if the divine Author is indeed still living, we pray and beseech him—that he might wish to commit his writings to the press himself, corrected and carefully refined according to his own mind; or, if he does not disdain to use us as humble servants <term>mediastinis</term><note>Latin: *mediastinus*, refers to a low-ranking helper or household drudge. The editor is positioning himself as a humble assistant to the author's genius.</note>, to ensure that the genuine manuscript reaches our hands in some way. We would consider it a glory to have corrected our own errors, having nothing ever more at heart than the **public good**. 

You, **Kind Reader**, will interpret these things in the best light, considering that if these things—like secret <term>encyclical</term><note>original Greek: *ἐγκύκλια* (enkyklia), meaning general or circular knowledge intended for a specific group.</note> and <term>traditional</term><note>original Greek: *πατροπαράδοτα* (patroparadota), meaning things handed down from one's ancestors or "the fathers."</note> teachings—had to wander about any longer under clandestine communications of handwritten copies, perhaps soon even more errors and gaps [would occur]...

<summary>The editor defends his minor corrections to the manuscript, expresses a humble desire for the original author to come forward with a perfect copy, and justifies the publication as being for the "public good."</summary>
<keywords>manuscript editing, authorship, philology, secret knowledge, traditional teachings, public good</keywords>

--- Page 19 ---
<meta>This page concludes the Editor’s Preface. It continues the discussion of editorial integrity, specifically justifying the corrections made to the manuscript to prevent the corruption of the author's original meaning.</meta>

->### To the Reader<-

...[lest] more enormous errors might have crept in, or the true meaning of the text itself might have been gradually obliterated—a fate that has befallen so many excellent treatises that I need not dwell on the matter here. I do not wish to add more at this point, as I am not attempting to write a full commentary. I felt it necessary to mention these things, if only for my own sake, lest the Author himself believe I have taken liberties with his work—as the "Straight Rope" <note>original Greek: *ὀρθὸς κάλως* (*orthos kalos*). This is a likely reference to the editor Andreas Orthelius, who used this pseudonym to publish controversial commentaries on the works of the famous alchemist Michael Sendivogius.</note> once presumed to do with the writings of **Sendivogius**—or lest others judge me unfavorably, as the world is now full of <term>sciolists</term><note>From the Latin *sciolus*, meaning those who possess superficial knowledge but pretend to be experts.</note>. 

In any case, my goal was to serve the **Chemical Arts** in this matter and to fulfill the desires of many who wished to see this work published. If I see that this effort is welcomed, perhaps more will follow; if not—well, I reject that ill omen. 

Greetings, **Kind Reader**, and farewell. 

Hamburg, on the Ides of December <note>December 13th.</note> in the year 1666.

<meta>catchword SYL; date: 1666</meta>

<summary>In this concluding section of the preface, the editor defends his corrections of the manuscript by citing the risk of textual corruption and distances himself from heavy-handed editors like Orthelius. He expresses a desire to serve the alchemical community and hints at future publications if this work is well-received.</summary>
<keywords>Michael Sendivogius, Andreas Orthelius, textual criticism, alchemy, chemical arts, Hamburg, 1666, sciolists</keywords>

# AN OPEN ENTRANCE

## TO

# THE CLOSED PALACE OF THE KING.

### AUTHOR’S PREFACE.

<image-desc>Decorative woodcut initial letter 'A' featuring foliage and floral motifs.</image-desc>Having attained <term>the secrets</term><note>original: *arcana*. Refers to the hidden knowledge of the Philosopher's Stone.</note> of medicine, chemistry, and physics myself—I, the anonymous <term>Philalethes</term><note>original: *Philaletha*. A pseudonym meaning "Lover of Truth." This author is historically identified as Eirenaeus Philalethes, likely the American chemist George Starkey.</note> and Philosopher—in the year 1645 of the redeemed world, and in the twenty-third year of my age, I decided to write this little treatise. I do this so that I may pay my debt to the <term>sons of art</term><note>A traditional term for dedicated students and practitioners of alchemy.</note> and reach out a hand to those entangled in the labyrinth of error. Through this work, it will appear to the <term>adepts</term><note>original: *adeptis*. Those who have already succeeded in the alchemical Great Work.</note> that I am their peer and brother; while those seduced by the trifles of <term>sophists</term><note>original: *sophistarum*. Fraudulent alchemists who used trickery or lacked true knowledge.</note> may see and embrace the light, by which they may safely return to the true path. I predict, furthermore, that many will be enlightened by these labors of mine. These are not fables, but real experiments which I have seen, performed, and known, as any adept will easily gather from these lines. Therefore, as I write these things for the good of my neighbor, let it suffice for me to declare that no one writing in this art has ever written so clearly. Indeed, I several times laid down my pen while writing, because I would have preferred to hide the truth under the <term>mask of envy</term><note>original: *invidiæ larva*. In alchemical literature, "envy" refers to the traditional secrecy kept by masters to prevent the unworthy from gaining power.</note>; but God compelled me, whom I could not resist, who alone knows hearts, and to whom alone be glory forever. From this I undoubtedly conclude that many in this final age of the world will be blessed by this secret. Because faithfully

I have written this, and I have not left the studious <term>novice</term><note>original: *tyroni*. A beginner or apprentice in the alchemical arts.</note> in any doubt that has not been perfectly satisfied. I also know that there are many who possess this secret along with me, and I am persuaded that there are many more with whom I shall shortly enter into a new familiarity, so to speak, day by day. May the holy will of **GOD** do what pleases Him; I confess myself unworthy that such things should be accomplished through me: nevertheless, in these matters I adore the holy will of **GOD**, to which all created things are bound to submit, for whose sake alone He created them, and protects them once created.

# C H A P T E R I.

## *On the necessity of Sophic Mercury for the work of the Elixir.*

Whoever desires to possess this <term>Golden Fleece</term><note>original: *vellere aureo*. A mythological reference commonly used by alchemists to symbolize the successful completion of the Great Work.</note>, let him know that our gold-making powder, which we call our <term>Stone</term><note>original: *lapidem nostrum*. The Philosopher's Stone.</note>, is simply gold digested into the highest degree of purity and subtle fixity to which it can be brought by nature and clever art. This gold, thus "essensified"—named <term>our gold</term><note>original: *aurum nostrum*. A distinction made between common metallic gold and the processed, philosophical gold used in the work.</note> (and no longer that of the common sort)—is the final pinnacle of perfection for both nature and art. I could cite all the <term>Philosophers</term><note>original: *Philosophos*. Here meaning historical alchemical authorities like Geber or Hermes Trismegistus.</note> on this matter; but I have no need for witnesses, because I am an <term>adept</term><note>original: *adeptus*. One who has successfully achieved the transmutation.</note> myself, and I write more clearly than anyone before this. Let him believe who will, let him disprove who can, let him criticize who likes. He will certainly carry away this reward: profound ignorance. Subtle, fa-

# TO THE CLOSED ENTRANCE OF THE KING'S PALACE.

<sig>A</sig>

...I confess, clever minds dream of fantasies; but in the simple way of nature, the diligent seeker will find the truth. Let gold, therefore, be the true, single, and only principle for making gold. Our gold, which we require for our work, is twofold: specifically the mature, fixed, "yellow Laton," <note>original: "Latonem flavum." Laton (referring to Latona, mother of Apollo/the Sun) is an alchemical term for the base or "impure" metal that must be cleansed to become pure gold.</note> whose heart or <margin>*</margin> center is pure fire. For this reason, it defends its body in the fire, wherein it receives purification, so that no part of it yields to the fire’s tyranny or suffers at its hands. In our work, this gold plays the part of the male; therefore, it is joined to our cruder "white gold," the female seed, into which it emits its own sperm. Finally, both unite in an unbreakable bond, and thus our **Hermaphrodite** <note>The alchemical "Hermaphrodite" or "Rebis" represents the perfect union of the male (Sulfur) and female (Mercury) principles.</note> is created, possessing the power of both sexes. Physical gold is therefore dead until it is joined with its <gloss>double</gloss> bride; through her, the coagulating sulfur <note>Sulfur was considered the active, "seeding" principle in the formation of metals.</note> that is on the outside of the gold is turned inward. In this way, what is high is hidden and what is deep is made manifest. Thus, the fixed [solid] becomes volatile [vaporous] for a time, so that it may later possess a more noble state by inheritance, in which it attains a superior stability. It is evident, then, that the entire secret lies in Mercury, <note>The "Mercury of the Philosophers" is a specialized solvent or substance, distinct from common quicksilver.</note> of whom the Philosopher says: "In Mercury," he says, "is whatever the wise seek." Regarding this, Geber <note>Geber is the Latinized name of Jabir ibn Hayyan, a foundational figure in alchemical history.</note> says: "Praised be the Most High, who created our Mercury and gave it a nature that overcomes all things." For certainly, unless this were present, Alchemists might boast as they please, but the Alchemical work would be in vain. It is clear, therefore, that this is not the common [mercury]...

<summary>This page defines the "twofold" nature of gold in the alchemical process, explaining how the male (gold/sulfur) and female (mercury) principles must unite to form a "Hermaphrodite" substance. It emphasizes that the secret of the Great Work lies in a specific, non-common form of Mercury.</summary>

# OPEN ENTRANCE <gloss>v-37</gloss>

<margin>37 here</margin>...lity. This is Mercury, but it is the Mercury of the Wise <note>original: "Sophicus." This refers to "Philosophical Mercury," a substance the alchemists believed was the hidden life-force within metals, distinct from the common quicksilver found in shops.</note>, because all the Mercury of the common people <margin>20</margin> is masculine—that is, it is bodily, fixed in a specific form, and "dead." But our Mercury is spiritual; it is a living and life-giving female. Pay attention, therefore, to what I am about to say concerning Mercury, because, as the Philosopher says, our Mercury is the Salt of the Wise <note>original: "Sal Sophorum." Salt was the third of the three prime principles (Mercury, Sulfur, Salt) in Paracelsian alchemy.</note>, without which, whoever works is like an archer who shoots without a bowstring—and yet this Mercury is nowhere to be found upon the <margin>32</margin> earth. However, the Son is formed by us—not by creating him from nothing, but by extracting him from those things in which he exists, with Nature cooperating in a wondrous way through shrewd art.

## CHAPTER II.

### *Concerning the component principles of Sophic Mercury.*

The intention of some working in this art is this: to purge Mercury in various ways. For some sublimate <note>To "sublimate" is to heat a substance until it turns to vapor and then cools back into a solid, a process used for purification.</note> it by adding salts; others separate it from various dregs; and others try to revivify it by itself alone. By these repeated operations, they claim to have made the Mercury of the Philosophers—and they err, because they do not work within Nature, which alone can be improved <margin>4</margin> within its own nature. Let them know, therefore, that our water <note>In alchemy, "water" often refers to the "universal solvent" or the metallic seed in a liquid state.</note> is composed of many things, yet it is one single thing made from different substances grown together <gloss>*</gloss> into one essence. Indeed, there is required in our water: first, fire; and second, the liquor of vegetable Saturnia <note>original: "liquor Saturniæ vegetabilis." "Saturn" is the alchemical name for Lead. Philalethes is likely referring to a "living" or "vegetable" (meaning growing/active) derivative of a lead-like mineral used as a solvent.</note>,<gloss>❧</gloss>

<summary>This page defines "Sophic Mercury" as a living, spiritual female principle distinct from common quicksilver and introduces Chapter II, which argues that this substance must be refined through nature rather than artificial chemical purifications.</summary>
<keywords>Sophic Mercury, Salt of the Wise, Alchemy, Sublimation, Nature, Vegetable Saturnia, Alchemical Principles</keywords>

--- Page 31 ---
<meta>This page continues the technical discourse on the "Sophic Mercury," moving from the preparation of the metallic heart (regulus) to describing the specific nature of the "Chaos"—the raw, intermediate state of the matter—and its mythological masks.</meta>

# TO THE CLOSED PALACE OF THE KING

Third is the bond of Mercury. The fire is of mineral <margin>A</margin> sulfur <gloss>Note</gloss>, and yet it is not properly mineral, much less metallic; but it is a middle state between ore and metal, neither one nor the other but partaking in both: a chaos or a spirit. This is because our fiery Dragon <note>A symbolic name for the corrosive acidic agent or the secret fire that "devours" the metals to transform them.</note>, which conquers all things, is nevertheless penetrated by the odor of the vegetable Saturnia <note>original: "Saturniæ vegetabilis." A specific preparation of antimony (associated with the planet Saturn) treated with an organic or "vegetable" spirit.</note>, whose blood congeals with the juice of Saturnia into one miraculous body. And yet it is not a body, because it is entirely volatile; <margin>B</margin> nor is it a spirit, because in the fire <margin>C</margin> it resembles a molten metal. It is therefore truly a chaos, which relates to all <margin>D</margin> metals as a mother. For from it I have learned to extract all things, even the Sun <note>Gold</note> and the Moon <note>Silver</note> without the transmuting Elixir, which whoever has seen the same can attest. This Chaos is called our arsenic <note>Not common arsenic, but the "Sophic" arsenic used as a philosophical solvent.</note>, our air, our Moon, our magnet, our steel—though in different respects, because our matter undergoes various states before the royal diadem is separated from the menses of our harlot <note>A provocative alchemical metaphor for the impure, "bloody" or colored byproduct of the initial purification process.</note>. Learn, therefore, who the companions of Cadmus <note>In mythology, Cadmus slew a dragon; here, he represents the alchemist purifying the matter.</note> <gloss>Note</gloss> are, and what the serpent is that devoured them, and what the hollow oak is, to which Cadmus <margin>F</margin> pinned the serpent. Learn what the doves of <margin>G</margin> Diana <note>Representing the "white" or silver-like stage of the work, often associated with spiritualization.</note> are, which conquer the Lion by soothing him—the <margin>H</margin> Lion, I say, the Green Lion, who is truly the <margin>I</margin> Babylonian Dragon, killing all things with its poison. Finally, learn the caduceus of Mercury <gloss>Note</gloss>, with which he works wonders, and who those nymphs are whom he infects by enchanting them, if you wish to obtain your desire.

<insert>+ through 7 digestions which as or Earths</insert> <note>This insertion appears to be a shorthand instruction regarding the number of "digestions" or heating cycles required for the process.</note>

## C H A P T E R III.

### *On the Steel of the Sages.*

The Wise Magi handed down many teachings about their **steel** <note>original: "chalybs." In alchemy, this is rarely common iron; it refers to a "magnetic" agent—usually a purified form of antimony—capable of "attracting" the spirit of gold.</note> to those who came after them, and they attributed no small importance to it. For this reason, there is no small conflict among the common crowd of **Alchemists** as to what exactly should be understood by the name of "steel." Various authors have given various interpretations of this. The **Author of the New Light** <note>Michael Sendivogius (1566–1636), author of "Novum Lumen Chymicum," a foundational text for 17th-century alchemists like Philalethes.</note> wrote about this candidly, yet obscurely. I, so that I might hide nothing from the seekers of this art out of envy, shall describe it sincerely. <margin>A</margin> Our steel is the true key of our work, without which the fire of the lamp cannot be lit by any art. It is the <gloss>*</gloss><gloss>6, 7, 8</gloss> **ore** of gold, a spirit very pure above all others. It is a secret, **infernal fire**, in its own kind highly volatile, a <margin>B</margin> miracle of the world, a system of the higher powers within the lower things. For this reason, the Almighty has marked it with a notable sign <note>This refers to the "Star of Antimony," a star-shaped crystalline pattern that appears on the surface of the metal when it is purified to a specific degree.</note>, whose birth is announced through the East on the <margin>C</margin> philosophical horizon of its own hemisphere. The wise men in the Age of the Magi saw it and were amazed, and they immediately recognized that a most serene <margin>D</margin> King was born into the world. You, when you have seen his stars, follow them even to the cradle; there you will see the beautiful infant. By removing the filth, honor the royal boy, <margin>E</margin> open your treasury, and offer the gift of gold. Thus at last, after death, <margin>F</margin> he will give you his flesh and blood, the highest **medicine** <note>The Philosopher's Stone, which was believed to "heal" base metals into gold and restore human health.</note> in the three kingdoms <note>original: "monarchiis." The mineral, vegetable, and animal kingdoms.</note> of the earth.

<summary>This page identifies "Sophic Steel" (a prepared form of antimony) as the essential catalyst for the alchemical work, using the Star of Bethlehem as a metaphor for the "starry" appearance of the purified metal.</summary>

# CHAPTER IV.

## On the Magnet of the Wise.

<margin>A</margin> **J**ust as steel <gloss>Iron or Mars</gloss> is drawn to the magnet, and the magnet spontaneously turns itself toward the steel, so also the Magnet of the Wise <term>Magnes Sophorum; a substance—often identified with purified antimony—that acts as a spiritual or physical attractor for the essence of gold</term> draws their steel. Therefore, just as I have taught that steel <gloss>Iron</gloss> is the ore of gold <gloss>the Sun</gloss>, likewise our Magnet <margin>B</margin> is the true ore of our steel. I further make it known that our Magnet has a hidden center abounding in Salt, which Salt is the solvent <term>menstruum; a liquid substance used to dissolve or extract essences from solids</term> in the Sphere <margin>C</margin> of the Moon <gloss>Silver</gloss>, which knows how to calcine <note>To reduce a substance to powder by heat, usually to remove volatile components</note> gold <gloss>the Sun</gloss>. This <margin>D</margin> center turns itself with an archetypal desire toward the Pole, <margin>E</margin> in which the power of the steel is exalted in degrees. <margin>F</margin> In the Pole <gloss>The "Star" or crystalline pattern on the metal's surface</gloss> is the heart of Mercury <term>Mercury; here referring to the "Philosophical Mercury" or the living essence of the metal</term>, which is the true fire, in <margin>G</margin> which is the rest of its Lord; let the one sailing through this great sea reach both the Indies <note>Referring to the "White" and "Red" works, the two primary goals of alchemy</note>, and <margin>H</margin> let him direct his course by the sight of the North Star, which our Magnet will make appear to you. The wise man will rejoice, yet the fool values these things little, nor will he learn wisdom, even though he has seen the <margin>I</margin> central pole turned outward and marked with the notable sign of the Almighty <note>A reference to the "star" that appears on the surface of purified antimony regulus, which the author views as a divine signature in nature</note>. They are so stiff-necked that even if they see signs and miracles, they do not lay aside their false arguments, nor do they enter the straight path.

# CHAPTER V.

## The Chaos of the Wise.

Let the Son of Philosophy hear the wise men unanimously concluding that this work is to be likened to the creation of the universe. *In the beginning, therefore, God created the heaven and the earth; and the earth was without form and void; and the Spirit of God moved over the face of the waters; and God said, "Let there be light," and there was light.* <note>original: "In initio igitur creavit DEUS cœlum & terram..." quoting the opening of Genesis from the Latin Vulgate bible</note> These words will be sufficient for a son of the Art <note>The "Art" is a common term for alchemy</note>. For indeed, heaven must be joined with earth upon the "bed of friendship." Thus shall he reign in honor throughout his whole life. <gloss>6. 7.</gloss> **Earth** is a heavy body, the womb <term>matrix; the environment or "mother-substance" in which minerals are formed</term> of minerals, because it secretly seethes within itself, although it brings forth trees and animals into the light. **Heaven** is that in which the great lights revolve together with the stars, and it sends down its powers through the air to the lower things; but in the beginning, all things, confused together, made a chaos.

Behold, I have sacredly revealed the truth: for our **Chaos** <term>Chaos; in alchemy, this refers to the "First Matter" (Prima Materia), the raw, unrefined substance at the start of the process</term> is like a mineral earth in respect to its coagulation <note>its solid or thickened state</note>, and yet it is a volatile air, within which is the heaven of the philosophers in its center. This center is truly astral <term>astral; meaning "star-like," referring to a celestial or spiritual energy trapped within the physical matter</term>, irradiating the earth even to its surface with its radiance. And what Magician <note>original: "Magus"</note> is so wise that from these things he might realize a new king is born, excelling all others, the redeemer of his brothers from their original stain? He must die and be raised on high, so that [he may give] his flesh and blood in...

<meta>catchword: of the world [mundi]</meta>

<summary>The author describes the "Chaos of the Wise" (the starting material of alchemy) as a microcosm of the biblical Creation, containing a hidden celestial radiance. He introduces the "New King"—a metaphor for the Philosopher's Stone—as a redeemer who must undergo a symbolic death and resurrection.</summary>

## CHAPTER VI.

->*The Air of the Sages.*<-

In the Sacred Scriptures, the expanse or firmament is called "air." Likewise, our <term>chaos</term><note>In alchemy, "chaos" often refers to the raw, unrefined matter of the Great Work, which contains all elements in a confused state.</note> is named "air," and this is not without a notable secret. Just as the air of the firmament acts as a separator of the waters <note>A reference to Genesis 1:6-7, where God creates the firmament to divide the waters above from the waters below.</note>, so too does our "air." Therefore, our work is truly a system of the macrocosm <note>original: "majoris mundi", meaning the greater world or universe.</note>. For just as the waters beneath the firmament are visible and apparent to us who live upon the earth, while the upper waters escape our sight because they are so far distant from us; so too in our <term>microcosm</term><note>The "little world," referring to the alchemical vessel or the human body as a reflection of the universe.</note>, there are "extracentral" mineral waters which are visible, but those which are shut within escape our sight, and yet they truly exist. These are the waters of which the Author of the *New Light* <note>original: "Autor Novi Luminis." This refers to Michael Sendivogius (1566–1636), a famous alchemist whose work "A New Light of Alchemy" was widely influential.</note> speaks: "they are, but they do not appear until it pleases the craftsman." Therefore, just as the atmospheric air distinguishes between the waters, so also does our "air" prohibit all entry of the outer mineral waters to the waters which are in the center. For if they were to enter...

<margin>A</margin> Therefore, I shall say that an external, vaporous, burning <term>sulfur</term><note>In this context, "sulfur" refers to an active, combustible principle that can either aid or corrupt the work depending on its purity.</note> adheres stubbornly to our <term>chaos</term><note>The "chaos" is the raw, prime matter of the alchemists, often described as a dark, confused substance containing the seeds of all things.</note>. ## CHAPTER VII.

### *Concerning the first operation of the preparation of Sophic Mercury by means of flying eagles.*

Know, brother, that the precise preparation of the eagles <note>In alchemical jargon, "eagles" usually refer to the process of sublimation or the number of times a substance is purified and "lifted" up</note> of the philosophers is considered the first step of perfection, in the understanding of which a skillful intellect is required. Do not believe that this knowledge has come to any of us by chance or through random imagination, as the stupidly ignorant common folk believe; instead, we have toiled much and long, spent many sleepless nights, and endured much labor and sweat to attain the truth.

Therefore, studious <term>Novice<note>original: "Tyro"; a beginner or apprentice in the alchemical arts</note></term>, know for certain that you will achieve nothing without sweat and labor—specifically in the "first work"—although in the second work, nature alone performs the task without any manual intervention, using only a moderate external fire. Therefore, brother, understand the sayings of the <term>wise ones<note>original: "sophorum"; referring to the Greek "sophoi," the esteemed masters of alchemy</note></term> when they write that their eagles must be led to devour the lion <note>The "lion" often symbolizes the fixed, metallic body (such as antimony or gold) which must be "eaten" or dissolved by the volatile mercury</note>; the fewer the number of these eagles, the harder the struggle and the slower the victory; however, the work is most excellently completed with the number seven or nine. This is, namely, Sophic Mercury...

<summary>This page introduces Chapter VII, emphasizing that alchemical success requires rigorous intellectual and physical labor rather than luck. It specifically discusses the "flying eagles"—a process of purifying mercury—and its interaction with the "lion."</summary>

->AN OPEN ENTRANCE<-

...-sophic bird of Hermes <note>original: "avis Hermetis"; a symbol for the volatile spirit or Mercury that can "fly" (evaporate) and "descend" (condense)</note>, which is now called a goose, now a pheasant, now this, now that. But when the Magi speak of their eagles, they speak in the plural number and assign a number from three up to ten. Yet they do not wish to be understood as if they require that many weights of water to one of earth; rather, it is necessary to interpret their words concerning internal weight. Namely, the water must be "sharpened" as many times as they count eagles; this sharpening is achieved through **sublimation** <note>The process of heating a substance until it turns to vapor and then becomes solid again, used here as a metaphor for purification</note>. Therefore, let each single sublimation of the Mercury of the Philosophers be considered one "eagle," and the seventh sublimation will so exalt your Mercury that it becomes a most suitable bath for your King.

¶ Therefore, so that you may have this knot properly untied, prick up your ears most attentively: Take four parts of our fiery Dragon <note>original: "Draconis nostri ignei"; likely referring to an acidic or "hungry" mineral ore like stibnite</note>, who hides magic steel <term>chalybs<note>A poetic/alchemical term for iron or the "strength" within a mineral</note></term> in his belly, and nine parts of our magnet <note>original: "magnetis"; not a literal magnet, but a substance that "attracts" the spirit, often iron or antimony</term>. From this will be produced the chameleon, or our "chaos," in which all secrets lie hidden by their power, though not yet in their finished state. ¶ This is the **Hermaphrodite infant**, who from its very first cradle was infected by the bite of the rabid Corascene dog <note>original: "canem Corascenum"; a famous and obscure alchemical metaphor for a corrosive or "biting" agent used to break down the metal</note>. Because of this, it grows foolish and goes mad with a perpetual hydrophobia <note>Fear of water; here, it refers to the substance's resistance to being dissolved or "wetted" by the philosophical water</note>, even though water is more closely related to it than any other natural thing...

# CHAPTER VIII.

->*On the labor and boredom of the first preparation.*<-

Certain ignorant alchemists <term>chemicolæ<note>A slightly dismissive term for those who practice the "chemical" side of the art without true philosophical understanding</note></term> dream that the entire work from beginning to end is mere recreation full of delight, and they believe that labor stands outside the boundaries of this art; let them enjoy their opinion in safety. In the work which they have so easily fashioned for themselves, they will surely reap a hollow harvest from their idle operations. We know, for instance, that after the divine blessing and a good root, labor, industry, and diligence hold the first place.

<summary>This page uses vivid allegories—the drowning of a rabid dog and the flight of an eagle—to describe the purification of Mercury. It concludes by debunking the idea that alchemy is effortless, stressing that true success requires hard work and diligence rather than "idle operations."</summary>

# OPEN ENTRANCE

...assiduity. Nor indeed will the "labor of the family" <note>original: "labor familiæ"; an alchemical term for the easier, domestic-scale tasks often contrasted with the grueling initial purification</note> (which should be considered child's play or a recreation of the mind) grant what we so greatly desire according to our prayers; on the contrary, as Hermes <note>Hermes Trismegistus, the legendary father of alchemy</note> says, one must spare neither soul nor labor. Otherwise, what the wise man predicted in parables will be verified: namely, that the desire of the idle man will kill him. Nor is it a wonder if so many men practicing Alchemy are reduced to poverty; they flee from labor, forsooth, and do not endure the costs.

But we, who know these things and have worked hard, have found for a certainty that no labor is more tedious than our first preparation. Therefore Morienus <note>Morienus Romanus, a 7th-century legendary alchemist known for his dialogue with King Khalid</note> seriously exhorts the King on this matter, saying: that very many of the wise have complained about the tedium of this work. And I would not wish these things to be understood only figuratively, since I am not now considering things as they appear at the start of the supernatural work, but as we first find them in their raw state. "To make the mass manageable," says the Poet <note>a reference to Virgil’s Aeneid</note>, "this is the work, this is the labor." And again:

> *One [seeks] the golden fleece from the well-known summit, etc.*
> *Another [shows] how much of a burden you must undergo and how much labor*
> *you must spend on the thick mass and the crude weight, etc.*

For that reason, that Noble Author of the Hermetic Secret <term>Arcanum Hermeticum<note>The "Secret of Hermes," referring to the hidden method of the Great Work</note></term> calls this first task a **Herculean labor**. For there are in our principles many heterogeneous superfluities <note>original: "heterogeneæ superfluitates"; impurities that are of a different nature than the desired metallic essence</note> which can never (for the purposes of our work) be reduced into purity; for that reason, it is expedient to purge them thoroughly, which will be impossible to do without our secrets...

...our theory, by which we teach the medium through which the **royal diadem** <note>original: "diadema regale"; a metaphor for the final Alchemical Stone or the purified essence of gold.</note> is separated from the <term>menstruum of the harlot<note>original: "meretricis menstruo"; an alchemical code term for a specific solvent or impure starting material, often associated with antimony.</note></term>. I do not deny, however, that a woman could easily undergo the labor of this art, provided that she counts these tasks as work rather than play. ### CHAPTER IX.

->*On the power of our Mercury over all metals.*<-

Our **Mercury** is that serpent which devoured the companions of **Cadmus**; and it is no wonder, for it had first devoured Cadmus himself, who was stronger than the rest. In the end, however, Cadmus will transfix this Serpent when he has coagulated it with the power of his **Sulphur**. Know, therefore, that this Mercury of ours dominates all metallic bodies, dissolving them into their proximate mercurial matter by separating their <term>Sulphurs<note>original: "Sulphura"; in alchemical theory, metals were composed of "Sulphur" (the active, combustible principle) and "Mercury" (the fluid, metallic principle).</note></term>. Know also that the Mercury of one, two, or three <term>eagles<note>original: "aquilae"; in this context, an "eagle" represents a single cycle of sublimation or distillation. The number of eagles indicates the "potency" or degree of purification of the mercury.</note></term> rules over **Saturn** [lead], **Jupiter** [tin], and **Venus** [copper]; it rules **Luna** [silver] from three eagles up to seven; and finally, it rules **Sol** [gold] with up to ten eagles.

## CHAPTER X.

### On the Sulphur which is in the Philosophers' Mercury.

AN OPEN ENTRANCE
<note>original: "INTROITUS APERTUS"; full title: *Introitus Apertus ad Occlusum Regis Palatium* (An Open Entrance to the Closed Palace of the King).</note>

## CAPUT XI.

### *Concerning the discovery of the perfect Mastery.*
<term>Mastery <note>original: "Magisterium"; the successful completion of the Great Work or the creation of the Philosopher's Stone.</note></term>

The wise men of old, as many as attained this art without the aid of books, were led to its achievement in this way: by the will of **GOD**. For I cannot persuade myself that it reached any by immediate revelation, unless perhaps Solomon possessed it—a matter I would rather leave to the reader's judgment than determine myself. And yet, even if he did possess it, what prevents him from having reached it by investigation <note>original: "indagine"; a systematic search or tracking down of a secret.</note>, since he requested only wisdom, which **GOD** gave to him in such a way that with it he also possessed wealth and peace? Since he explored the nature of plants and trees, from the Cedar in Lebanon even to the hyssop that grows upon the wall <note>A reference to 1 Kings 4:33, highlighting Solomon's reputation as the ultimate naturalist and sage.</note>, who of sound mind will deny that he likewise understood the natures of minerals? The knowledge of these is no less pleasant. 

But to the matter at hand. We say that it is likely to be believed that the first <term>adepts <note>original: "adeptos"; those who have "attained" the secret of the transmutation.</note></term> who possessed this Mastery—among whom was Hermes <note>Hermes Trismegistus, the legendary Egyptian figure to whom the origins of alchemy are attributed.</note>—and who lacked an abundance of books, sought at first no more than perfection. They sought only the simple exaltation of imperfect things to the royal state <note>The "royal state" refers to gold, the most "perfect" and stable of all metals.</note>. When they perceived that all metallic things are of <term>Mercurial <note>original: "Mercurialis"; the theory that all metals are composed of a fundamental "Mercury" or "Quick-silver" combined with "Sulfur."</note></term> origin, and that **Mercury** is most similar to gold—the most perfect of metals—in its weight and homogeneity...

<meta>catchword: this</meta>

<summary>The author argues that the first alchemists, including Solomon and Hermes, discovered the secrets of nature through divine favor and a systematic study of minerals rather than through textbooks. He begins to explain that their discovery started with the observation that Mercury is the foundational substance of all metals.</summary>

<keywords>Solomon, Hermes Trismegistus, Mastery, Mercury, origin of metals, divine revelation, alchemical history</keywords>

# TO THE CLOSED PALACE OF THE KING.
## B 2

They have therefore attempted to digest this <note>referring to Mercury</note> to the maturity of gold, but they could not achieve it by any fire. Wherefore they considered within themselves that, besides external heat, at least an internal <gloss>fire</gloss> was required to fulfill their desires. And so they sought this in many things. First, they distilled highly hot waters from lesser minerals, and with the same they corroded Mercury; but by no art in this way could they cause Mercury to change its intrinsic properties, inasmuch as all corrosive waters were only external agents, after the manner of fire, though differently; and these <term>menstrua <note>original: "menstrua"; alchemical solvents or "mother liquids" used to dissolve solids.</note></term>, as they called them, did not remain with the dissolved body. 

Confirmed by the same reasoning, they rejected all salts, except for one salt, which is the <term>first entity <note>original: "ens primum"; the essential or most primordial state of a substance.</note></term> of salts <gloss>34</gloss>, which dissolves any metal, and by the same operation coagulates Mercury; but this only by a violent way. <margin>D</margin> Wherefore an agent of this kind is again separated from things with its weight and strength intact. 

Wherefore the wise men finally recognized that in Mercury, aqueous crudities and earthy dregs stand in the way of it being digested; these are so deeply rooted that they cannot be exterminated except through <margin>E</margin> an inversion of the whole compound. They knew, I say, that if Mercury could cast these off, it would immediately become fixed. For it has within itself a <term>fermental sulfur <note>In alchemy, "sulfur" represents the active, masculine, and combustible principle that "fixes" or solidifies the fluid Mercury.</note></term>, of which even the smallest grain would be capable of coagulating the entire Mercurial body, provided the dregs and crudities could be removed. This they therefore attempted by purifica- <note>The text ends mid-word: "purgatio-"; the catchword "nibus" completes it to "purgationibus," meaning purifications or cleansings.</note>

<meta>catchword: nibus</meta>

...in various ways, but in vain; seeing as the aforementioned work requires mortification <note>In alchemy, "mortification" refers to the symbolic death or breakdown of a substance, usually involving a change in color to black.</note> and regeneration alike, for which there is a need for an Agent. <margin>A</margin> And at last they knew that Mercury in the bowels of the earth was destined to become a metal, toward which goal <margin>4</margin> it maintained a daily motion as long as the fitness of the place and other external factors remained well-disposed; but when these were corrupted by chance, this immature offspring spontaneously fell away. Thus, because it is seen as deprived of a certain motion and life—and a return from privation to a state of being is impossible immediately. That which ought to have been active is, you see, passive in Mercury; so that it is necessary to introduce another life, of the same nature, into it, by the introduction of which it awakens the latent life of the Mercury. ...striving [to purge it], they found it entirely impossible, because it contained no metallic sulphur within itself, <margin>A</margin> though it abounded in the most purified **salt of nature**. That which they noted in **Mercury** was only a meager and passive sulphur; in this **offspring of Saturn** <note>original: "Saturni prole"; a common alchemical name for Antimony</note>, they found no actual sulphur, but only potential. Therefore, it enters into a league with a burning **arsenical sulphur**, and without this, it becomes foolish and cannot subsist in a coagulated form; and yet it is so foolish that it prefers to live with this enemy—by whom it is most tightly imprisoned—and to commit fornication, rather than to renounce it and appear in a **Mercurial form**.

Therefore, seeking an active sulphur further, the <term>Sages</term> <note>original: "Magi"; referring to the master alchemists</note> finally sought and found it hidden most deeply in the **House of the Ram** <note>original: "domo arietis"; Aries is the astrological house of Mars, symbolizing Iron</note>. This [sulphur] was most greedily received by the offspring of Saturn, which—being a metallic matter most pure, most tender, and closest to the first metallic being—is deprived of all actual sulphur, yet holds the potential to receive it. Wherefore, like a magnet, it draws this to itself, and absorbs and hides it in its belly; and the Almighty, in order to greatly adorn this work, imprints **His royal seal** <note>original: "regium suum sigillum"; this refers to the "Star Regulus," a star-shaped crystalline pattern that forms on the surface of antimony when it is purified with iron</note> upon it.

Then the Sages immediately rejoiced when they beheld the sulphur not only found, but even prepared. Finally, they attempted to purge **Mercury** through this, but the outcome did not correspond [to their hopes], because the arsenical malignancy was still mixed with this sulphur absorbed in the offspring of Saturn—

### CAPUT XII.

->*On the method of performing the perfect*<-
->*Mastery in general.*<-

We ought to give immortal thanks to GOD, because he has shown us these <term>secrets of nature</term> <note>original: "arcana naturæ"</note>, which he has hidden from the eyes of most. Therefore, what has been given to us freely by that great Giver, <margin>Freely</margin> we shall reveal freely and faithfully to other scholars. Know, therefore, that the greatest secret of our operation consists of nothing else than the <term>cohobation</term> <note>original: "cohobationem"; the process of repeatedly distilling a liquid from its own solid remains to increase its potency.</note> of natures, one upon the other, until the most <term>digested</term> <note>original: "virtus digestissima"; referring to the maturity or refinement of the substance.</note> virtue is extracted from the digested [substance] by the raw.

To this, however, is required first: the exact comparison, preparation, and adaptation of the things entering the work. Second: <margin>2</margin> a good disposition of external things; third: once the things are thus prepared, a good <term>regimen</term> <note>original: "regimen"; the careful management of heat and conditions over time.</note> is required; fourth: a foreknowledge of the signs appearing in the work is desired, so that one does not proceed blindly; fifth: patience, so that the work is not hurried or managed headlong. Of all these things, we shall speak in order, as a brother to a brother.

<summary>This page concludes a description of the soul's exaltation into the quintessence and begins Chapter XII, which outlines the five essential requirements for the alchemical Mastery: preparation, external conditions, regimen, foreknowledge, and patience.</summary>

# CHAPTER XIII.

->*On the use of mature Sulphur in the work of the Elixir.*<-

**WE** have spoken of the necessity of <term>Mercury</term> <note>In alchemy, Mercury represents the fluid, feminine, and life-giving principle, not just the liquid metal.</note>, and we have handed down many secrets about Mercury which, before me, were quite meager in the world. This is because almost all chemical books swarm either with obscure riddles, deceptive operations, or finally a heap of "scabby" words <note>original: "verborum scabiosorum"; meaning rough, crude, or confusing language intended to hide the truth.</note>. I, however, have not acted thus, resigning my true will in this matter to the divine pleasure of Him who seems to me about to reopen these treasures in this final period of the world. Therefore, I no longer fear lest the art become cheapened—far from it. This cannot happen. For true wisdom protects itself in eternal honor. 

Would that gold and silver finally became as cheap as dung!—that great idol worshipped by the whole world until now! Then we, who are skilled in these things, would not strive so much to hide. As it stands, we judge ourselves to have already received (mourning and sighing!) the very <term>curse of Cain</term> <note>referring to Genesis 4:12, where Cain is condemned to be a "fugitive and a wanderer." Alchemists often claimed that possessing the secret of the Stone made them targets for greedy rulers, forcing them into a life of constant flight.</note>; namely, that we are driven away, as it were, from the face of the Lord, and from the pleasant society which we once had with friends without fear. Now indeed we are driven about as if possessed by furies, nor can we believe ourselves safe in any place for long; often too, complaining, we make Cain's lamentation to GOD. Behold

<meta>The catchword "qui-" suggests the next page begins with a word like "quisquis" (whoever) or "quicumque."</meta>

"whoever finds me, will kill me" <note>original: "quicunque me inveniet, occidet"; a direct quotation of Cain from Genesis 4:14, illustrating the author's fear of being hunted for his secrets.</note>. Not daring to take up the care of a family, as **wanderers**, we roam through various nations, nor do we obtain any certain habitation. And although we possess all things, yet we are permitted to use few; in what then are we happy, except in <term>speculation</term> <note>original: "speculatione"; here meaning the intellectual and spiritual contemplation of the Great Work rather than financial risk.</note> alone, in which there is great satisfaction of the mind. Many believe, who are strangers to the **art**, that if they possessed it, they would do these and such things; so too we once believed, but having become more cautious through dangers, we have chosen a more secret method. For he who has once escaped imminent danger to his life, for the rest, believe me, will be rendered wiser as long as he lives. Wives, as the proverb goes, of bachelors, and the children of virgins, are well dressed and educated <note>A variation of the proverb "Bachelors' wives and maids' children are always well taught," suggesting that those who do not have a responsibility find it easy to criticize those who do.</note>. I have found the world placed in a most malignant state, so that almost no one can be found—however much he wears a face of honesty and displays a concern for public affairs—who does not propose some private, sordid, and unworthy goal for himself. Nor is any mortal alone able to effect anything, as indeed in works of mercy, unless he wishes to incur the risk of his head <note>meaning the risk of execution or capital punishment.</note>: which I recently experienced in certain foreign places, where I offered <term>medicine</term> to certain dying, deserted people afflicted with bodily miseries, and they recovered their health miraculously; a murmur was immediately made about the <term>Elixir of the Wise</term> <note>original: "Elixiri Sophorum"; the "Universal Medicine" or Philosopher's Stone in liquid form, believed to cure all ailments.</note>, so that not once, with the greatest troubles, with changed clothes, a shaved head, and other [false] hair in-

# THE OPEN ENTRANCE
->THE OPEN ENTRANCE<-

...led, taking a changed name, I fled by night; otherwise, I would have fallen into the hands of the most wicked men lying in wait for me (on account of mere suspicion joined with the fiercest thirst for gold). I could relate many things of this sort, which will seem ridiculous to some. For they will say, if I knew this or that, I would act differently; let them know, however, that it is tedious for the ingenious to associate with the slow-witted. The ingenious, moreover, are crafty, subtle, and perceptive; some are as many-eyed as **Argus** <note>original: "Argi"; refers to Argus Panoptes, the hundred-eyed giant of Greek myth who could see everything.</note>, some <gloss>a</gloss> are curious, and some are **Machiavellian** <note>original: "Machiavelliani"; referring to the political cunning and pragmatism associated with Niccolò Machiavelli.</note>, who inquire into the life, character, and actions of men most deeply. From such as these, it is very difficult to remain hidden, at least if there is any familiar acquaintance. 

If, addressing such a man who believes these things about himself (namely, that he would act in this way or that if he possessed the <term>stone</term> <note>original: "lapide"; the Philosopher's Stone, the goal of the Great Work.</note>), I were to say: "You are an acquaintance of a certain <term>adept</term> <note>original: "adepti"; one who has mastered the secrets of alchemy.</note>," he would immediately turn it over in his mind and reply: "This is impossible; perhaps I might see one once, but to associate familiarly with him—it is not possible but that I would smell him out." 

You, who believe these things about yourself, do you not believe that others possess equal perspicacity with you, who might discern you? For it is necessary to associate with some people, otherwise you will seem like another **Diogenes the Cynic** <note>original: "Cynicus alter Diogenes"; the Greek philosopher famous for living in a tub and rejecting social norms.</note>. But if you associate with the common people, this is unworthy. If, however, you contract a familiarity among the prudent, you must be exceedingly cautious, lest others discern you with the same ease with which you believe you could fish out another adept (who knows the secret unknown to you), provided that by familiar

Such is the wickedness in men that we have known some to be strangled by a noose who were nevertheless strangers to the Art <note>original: "ab arte," referring here to alchemy</note>. It was enough that certain desperate men had heard a rumor about such an art, for which they had a reputation of expertise. It would be tedious to review everything that we ourselves have experienced, seen, and heard regarding this matter—moreover, in this age of the world, more than in any previous one. Who does not profess Alchimy <term>Alchimia: Alchemy, the ancient art of transmutation and medicine</term>, so much so that you would scarcely dare to move a foot unless you wish to be betrayed, if only you have transacted something in secret. This caution of yours will strike a zeal into some to examine you more deeply; they will snarl about the "sophistication" <term>sophisticatione: in this context, the adulteration or illegal counterfeiting of coins</term> of coins, and what not? But if you are a little more open, the effects are unusual, whether they be in Medicine or in Alchemy; if you possess a huge weight of gold and silver and wish to sell it, anyone will easily wonder from where such a great quantity of refined gold <term>auri obryzi: gold that has been tested and proved pure by fire</term> or the purest silver is being brought, since refined gold is brought from almost no places except perhaps *Barbary* <note>The Barbary Coast of North Africa</note> or *Guinea* <note>West Africa, a major source of gold in the 17th century</note>, and even then in the form of the finest sand: yours, however, being of a nobler grade than that, and yet in the form of a mass <note>i.e., an ingot or solid bar</note>, will not be free from the greatest gossip. Buyers are not so stupid, even if, playing like children, they might say, "Our eyes are closed; come, we do not see"; if you arrive

These laws provide that every debasement of gold and silver—even if it is brought to the legal standard <term>stateram: the official weight or purity standard</term>—is considered a capital crime if not performed by a professional and licensed metalsmith. 

We ourselves know this, because when we once attempted to sell as much as 600 pounds of the purest silver outside our own country, dressed as merchants—for we did not dare to debase it, because almost every region has its own standard of quality for silver and gold, which the Metal-workers <term>Metallici: assayers, goldsmiths, or those skilled in testing metals</term> recognize easily—we were in danger. If we had pretended it was brought from here or there, they would have immediately recognized the deception through testing <note>original: "per probam," referring to the cupellation or assaying process</note> and seized the seller. Those to whom we offered it immediately told us that it was silver "made by art." When we asked why they claimed this, they responded nothing else than that the silver which is brought from England, Spain, etc. is...

...[we are] not now about to learn how to discern; but this is of none of those kinds. Hearing this, we secretly withdrew ourselves, and we left behind both the silver and the price, never to be reclaimed. Moreover, if you imagine a great weight of gold, and especially of silver, brought from elsewhere, this cannot happen without rumor. The ship’s master <note>original: "nauclerus"; the captain or owner of a merchant vessel</note> will say, "Such a quantity of silver was not brought by me," nor can it enter a ship without everyone’s knowledge. And when others who are accustomed to trade there hear of it, they will laugh and say, "What? Is it likely that he could obtain a mass of silver or gold here and place it on a ship, when the laws are so strictly prohibitive and such strict scrutiny is usually practiced as a precaution?" Thus, it will immediately be made public, not just in one region, but in all those surrounding it.

We, having been taught by such dangers, have decided to remain hidden, and we will communicate these things to you—who dream of such an art <note>The "Art" refers to Alchemy, the pursuit of transmuting base metals into gold</note>—so that we may see what you will devise for the public good <note>original: "bonum publicum"</note> once you have attained it. We say, therefore, that just as I previously taught that Mercury <term>Mercurium; in alchemy, this represents the fluid, feminine, and volatile principle of matter</term> is necessary in the work, and I brought forth such things about Mercury as no antiquity before me has done; so now I make known that Sulphur <term>Sulphur; in alchemy, this represents the active, masculine, and combustible principle</term> is required on the other hand. Without this Sulphur, Mercury will never receive a profitable thickening <note>original: "congelationem"; refers to the "fixation" or hardening of a liquid substance into a solid state</note> for the supernatural work. This Sulphur plays the role of the male in our work, and without it, whoever approaches the art of transmutation <note>original: "artem transmutatoriam"</note> attempts everything in vain, for all...

<meta>This page continues the discussion from page 29 regarding the theoretical principles of alchemy. While the previous page focused on the logistical dangers of possessing gold and introduced the role of Mercury and Sulphur, page 30 shifts to the specific nature of "Philosophical Gold" and how it must be "re-awakened" to act as a seed for transmutation. ### AN OPEN ENTRANCE

The Sages affirm that no **tincture** <note>A "tincture" in alchemy is a potent substance—often the Elixir or Philosopher's Stone—capable of infusing its qualities into other metals to transmute them.</note> can be produced 
<margin>A</margin> without their **copper**, which "copper" is, without any ambiguity, a name for **gold**. On this point, the noble Sendivogius <note>Michael Sendivogius (1566–1636) was a famous Polish alchemist and pioneer of chemistry.</note> says: "The one who knows," he says, "recognizes our Stone even among dung, while the one who is ignorant does not believe it is present even in gold." 
<margin>B</margin> In gold—that is, the **Gold of the Sages** <term>aurum Sophorum; not common currency gold, but a purified "living" metallic soul used in the Work</term>—the **tincture of goldenness** lies hidden. Although this is a most highly 
<margin>C</margin> digested <note>In alchemy, "digestion" refers to the process of maturing or perfecting a substance through gentle heat.</note> body, it is nevertheless **returned to a raw state** <note>original: "incrudatur"; the vital alchemical process of breaking down a "perfect" metal back into its primary, "crude" matter so it can be transformed.</note> in our **Mercury** alone. From this Mercury, it receives the multiplication of its seed, not so much in weight as in power. And although many of the Sages seem to deny this as a "Sophistry" <note>A false or deceptive argument; here meaning a fake alchemical process.</note>, it is truly as I have said. 

They say, for example, that common gold is "dead," but theirs is "alive." In the same way, a **grain of wheat** is "dead"—meaning the germinating activity within it lies suppressed and dormant—and it would remain so forever if it were kept in the surrounding dry air. But if it is cast into the earth, it soon receives a **fermental life**; it swells, softens, and germinates. 

So it is with our gold: it is "dead," meaning its **life-giving force** <note>original: "vis vivifica"</note> is sealed under a bodily husk, much like a grain—though there is a great difference between a vegetable grain and metallic gold. But just as a grain remains forever unchanged in dry air and is destroyed by fire, it is only reducible in our **water** <note>Likely referring to the "Philosophical Mercury," often called "water" because of its liquid, solvent nature.</note>, and then our grain is "alive"; just as wheat...
